Simplicial sheaves of modules and Morita invariance of groupoid cohomology
Xiang Tang, Joel Villatoro

Abstract
. In this article we develop a unified framework for proving Morita invariance of cohomology theories associated to Lie groupoids. Our approach is to view these cohomology theories as arising from sheaves of modules on the nerve of the groupoid. We establish criteria for when such sheaves of modules give rise to Morita invariant cohomology theories.



1.
Introduction

Briefly, a groupoid is a small category with invertible morphisms. More concretely, a groupoid consists of two sets
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
called the set of
objects
and the set of
arrows
, respectively. These two sets are equipped with the following additional structure:

•

The source
s^{\mathcal{G}}
and target
t^{\mathcal{G}}
are functions from
\mathcal{G}_{1}
to
\mathcal{G}_{0}
mapping a morphism
g\in\mathcal{G}
to elements in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. From a category theoretic point of view, the source and target functions represent the domain and codomain, respectively.

•

The multiplication
m
is a function from
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}=\mathcal{G}_{1}{{}_{s}\times_{t}}\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{G}_{1}
mapping a pair of composable morphisms, i.e.
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
such that
s(g_{1})=t(g_{2})
, to the composition
g_{1}g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
.

•

The identity morphism, which is a function
i:\mathcal{G}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}
.

•

The inverse of a morphism gives a bijection from
\mathcal{G}_{1}
to itself.

Note that if the set of objects
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is a singleton, then this data is equivalent to the data needed to define a group.

Similar to Lie groups, Lie groupoids are groupoids
\mathcal{G}
where both
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
are smooth manifolds
1
1

1

\mathcal{G}_{1}
might not be Hausdorff.
with smooth structure maps
2
2

2

We often require that both
s^{\mathcal{G}}
and
t^{\mathcal{G}}
are submersions.
. Lie groups are useful as very general models for studying the symmetries of a space. From this point of view, one thinks of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
as encoding symmetry data over the collection of objects
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. For example, if a Lie group
G
acts on a smooth manifold
M
then we can form a Lie groupoid with arrows
\mathcal{G}_{1}:=G\times M
and objects
\mathcal{G}_{0}:=M
.

Since Lie groupoids represent very general models for symmetry, they are also useful as models for the study quotient spaces where we take the quotient of a manifold
M
via some prescribed symmetry. Indeed, given a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, we can say two elements
x,y\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
are equivalent, denoted by
x\sim y
, if there is a morphism
g\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
such that
x=s(g)
and
y=t(g)
. This is to be thought of as the equivalence relation induced on
\mathcal{G}_{0}
arising from symmetries encoded by
\mathcal{G}
. For example, in the case of a
G
action on a manifold
M
the equivalence classes on
\mathcal{G}_{0}
are precisely the orbits of the
\mathcal{G}
action.

In general, we call the equivalence classes of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
the
orbits
of the Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}_{0}/\sim
is called the
orbit space
. Now, in general, the orbit space
\mathcal{G}_{0}/\sim
is not a manifold and can have very coarse topology (few continuous functions). The orbit space
\mathcal{G}_{0}/\sim
is sometimes called the
coarse moduli space
and is often a rather unsatisfying model for the singular space. An alternative to this purely topological approach to the quotient arises from viewing Lie groupoids as
differentiable stacks
. The idea behind the differentiable stack approach is that one should treat the entire Lie groupoid as a sort of atlas for the quotient space. The notion of Morita equivalence of Lie groupoids is defined
3
3

3

There are several equivalent characterizations of Morita equivalence (see
[
BX11
]
). One version says two groupoids are Morita equivalent if they have equivalent categories of principle bundles. Another version says Lie groupoids are Morita equivalent if there exists a “smooth” equivalence of categories between them (see Sec.
6.1
).
in a way that, philosophically speaking, means two Lie groupoids are Morita equivalent when they are both atlases for “isomorphic” differentiable stacks. Towards this end, it is interesting to study properties of Lie groupoids that are preserved by Morita equivalence (i.e. Morita invariants) as they are thought of as natural invariants associated to the underlying singular space.

This article is concerned with studying Morita invariants that arise from various natural cochain complexes that one can associate to Lie groupoids. The most fundamental of these cochain complexes is simply called the
groupoid cohomology
which is formed from the set of
groupoid cochains
(see Definition
2.8
). This complex (and hence its cohomology) is thought of as a model for the algebra of functions on the orbit space. In fact, when a Lie groupoid is Morita equivalent to a smooth manifold (a space equipped with only trivial symmetries), this complex is quasi-isomorphic to the vector space of functions on the quotient space.

Moving beyond groupoid cochains, there are other natural complexes and cohomologies that come from closely related structures. For example, one can extend groupoid cochains to groupoid cochains with values in a representation of a Lie groupoid on a vector bundle or some weak version thereof. This complex provides us with a model for the space of sections of a vector bundle over the quotient space. Another example is the Bott-Shulmann cohomology of a Lie groupoid, which provides a model for the deRham cohomology of the underlying singular space. As natural generalizations of group cohomology, groupoid cohomologies are well studied in literature with applications in the study of gerbes,
C^{*}
-algebras, dynamical systems, Lie theory, etc., c.f.
[
BX11
,
Hae79
,
MW95
,
Ren80
]
. In particular, there are many articles in the literature concerned with proving Morita invariance of these and other closely related cohomologies, c.f.
[
AC13
,
CM04
,
dHOS24
,
LBM15
,
MS20
,
WX91
,
Tu06
]
.

The aim of this article is to provide a unified framework for proving that these kinds of cohomology theories are Morita invariants. Rather than having many different specialized approaches adapted to each context, in this article, we will show that one can establish the Morita invariance of these different cohomology theories by viewing them as different examples of one structure: a well-behaved sheaf of complexes over the nerve of a groupoid. We will give a set of conditions which are sufficient to conclude that the cohomology of such a sheaf of complexes indeed constitutes a Morita invariant.

Main Results

In order to state our main results, let us establish some terminology and observations:

•

A smooth homomorphism of Lie groupoids
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is called a
weak equivalence
if its an equivalence of categories and
F_{0}\colon\mathcal{G}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
is transverse to the orbits of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. Two Lie groupoids are Morita equivalent if and only if they can be connected by a zig-zag of weak equivalences
4
4

4

For the purposes of this article this is essentially the
definition
of Morita equivalence.
.

•

A
cosimplicial complex
on a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
consists of a sheaf of complexes defined on the nerve of
\mathcal{G}
which has a (co)simplicial structure compatible with the existing simplicial structure on
\mathcal{G}
. Such cosimplicial complexes are called
good
if they satisfy a sort of Kan-like condition (see Definitions
7.8
and Definition
9.5
). Every cosimplicial complex has a natural cohomology associated to it that we denote with
H(\mathcal{E})
.

•

A
sheaf of complexes on the big site

\mathcal{E}
is a sheaf on the site of smooth manifolds which assigns to each manifold
M
a complex
\mathcal{E}(M)
of
C^{\infty}_{M}
-modules (think, for example, the sheaf of differential forms
\Omega
of all degrees).

•

Given a homomorphism of Lie groupoids
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
and a cosimplicial complex
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{G}
then it is possible to construct a “pull-back” cosimplicial complex
F^{*}\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{H}
.

We can now state our main theorems:

Theorem 1.1

(From Theorem
9.9
in body)
.

Suppose
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence of Lie groupoids and
\mathcal{E}
is sheaf of complexes on the big site. Then
H(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}))\cong H(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H}))
.

Theorem 1.2

(From Theorem
9.11
in body)
.

Suppose
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence of Lie groupoids and
\mathcal{E}
is a good cosimplicial complex on
\mathcal{G}
. Let
F^{*}\mathcal{E}
denote the pullback cosimplicial complex on
\mathcal{G}
. Then:

H(\mathcal{E})\cong H(F^{*}\mathcal{E})

Several well-known results regarding Morita invariant cohomologies follow from these theorems as corollaries. Our first two corollaries relate the complexes of groupoid cochains of Morita equivalent groupoids.

Corollary 1.3

(
[
Cra03
]
5
5

5

This article is the earliest appearance of this fact in the literature as far as the authors are aware.
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid and
\Ch(\mathcal{G})
denote the complex of groupoid cochains on
\mathcal{G}
. If
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
are Morita equivalent Lie groupoids then we have that:

H(\Ch(\mathcal{G}))\cong H(\Ch(\mathcal{H}))

Corollary 1.4

(Originally proved in
[
AC13
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid and suppose we have a representation of
\mathcal{G}
on a vector bundle
E\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
. Let
\Ch(\mathcal{G},E)
be the associated cochain complex of
\mathcal{G}
-cochains with coefficients in
E
. If
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence and
\Ch(\mathcal{H},F^{*}E)
is the cochain complex of
F^{*}E
-valued cochains then we have that:

H(\Ch(\mathcal{G},E))\cong H(\Ch(\mathcal{H},F^{*}E))

Another corollary relates the cohomologies of the Bott-Schulman double complex of Morita equivalent Lie groupoids.

Corollary 1.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid and let
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}^{(\bullet)})
be the Bott-Schulman double complex. Write
H_{BS}(\mathcal{G})
to denote the total cohomology of this double complex. If
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
are Morita equivalent groupoids then:

H_{BS}(\mathcal{G})\cong H_{BS}(\mathcal{H})

Our last corollary is more of a corollary of the proofs than the actual conclusions of Theorem 1.1 and 1.2. It says that the conclusions of these theorems still hold if we pass to the appropriate notion of compactly supported cohomology.

Corollary 1.6
.

Given
\mathcal{E}
an arbitrary cosimplicial complex over
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{C}
denote the (pre)sheaf of sections of
\mathcal{E}
which are compactly supported (relative to the topology on the orbit space
\mathcal{G}_{0}/\mathcal{G}_{1}
. Then
\mathcal{E}_{C}
is a subcomplex with associated cohomology denoted
H_{C}(\mathcal{E})
.

Theorem 1.1 still holds if we replace the last equation with:

H_{C}(\mathcal{E})\cong H_{C}(F^{*}\mathcal{E})

and Theorem 1.2 still holds if we replace the last equation with:

H_{C}(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}))\cong H_{C}(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H}))

Our Approach

Our philosophy is to approach the topic in a relatively low-tech way. In particular, we wish to write the article in a way accessible to those mainly familiar with the smooth theory of Lie groupoids without relying on higher structures more than necessary. The primary tools we will use come from the structure and theory of simplicial manifolds and cosimplicial vector spaces.

Much of what we discuss in this article can be generalized beyond the context of Lie groupoids and can be adapted to the study of simplicial manifolds (satisfying some appropriate Kan condition). However, fully extending our work would require a bit of theoretical development beyond what we present here. This could be the topic of a future article if there is interest.

A key component of our approach is the notion of a Decalage (or the shift double of a simplicial set). The shift double of a simplicial set is a way of converting any simplicial set into a homotopy equivalent (and relatively simple) double simplicial set. The proofs of our main theorems rely on showing that the associated morphism at the level of complexes factors through the shift double in a “nice” way.

The article is organized as follows. A brief introduction to the background and notations is included in Section
2
. We explain the Decalages construction in Section
3
. In Section
4
, we discuss retracts of simplicial manifolds and cosimpicial vector spaces. The discussion of shifted retractions of groupoids is presented in Section
5
. In Section
6
, we explain the double groupoid associated with a groupoid homomorphism. Cohomology of sheaves of cosimplicial modules on a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is introduced in Section
7
. Morita invariance of cohomology of sheaves of cosimplicial modules on groupoids, Theorem
8.3
, is established in Section
8
. We prove a generalized version of Morita invariance of the Bott-Shulman cohomology via sheaves of cosimplicial complexes on groupoids (Theorem
9.11
), in Section
9
.

: We would like to thank

2.
Background and Notation

2.1.
Lie groupoids notation

If
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid we write
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{0}
to denote the arrows and objects respectively. We use the notation:

\mathcal{G}^{(n)}:=\overbrace{\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\cdots\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}_{1}}^{n\text{-times}}

to denote the space of
n
composable arrows.

Many of the objects we consider will have their own notions of “source” and “target.” If necessary to disambiguate we may use superscripts
\mathbf{s}^{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
or
\mathbf{t}^{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
to indicate we are specifically referring to the source map (or target map) for the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
. However, in order to reduce clutter in our notation we may often omit these superscripts when it is clear from context. We will also consider the source and target maps for a groupoid to be defined for tuples of composable arrows with the convention:

\mathbf{s}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1}):=\mathbf{s}(g_{1})\qquad\mathbf{t}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1}):=\mathbf{t}(g_{n})

Hence, for example, given a smooth function
f\colon N\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
we have that:

\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{f}}}}N=\{(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1},x)\in\mathcal{G}^{n}\times N\ :\ (g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\text{ and }\mathbf{s}(g_{1})=f(x)\}

2.2.
Simplicial and cosimplicial structures

The sets of composable arrows of a Lie groupoid have a special structure which is formally referred to as a simplicial set (or manifold in the case of a Lie groupoid).

Definition 2.1
.

A
simplicial set

S
consists of the following data:

•

a collection of sets indexed by natural numbers
\{S^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
;

•

for each
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, a function:

d^{n}_{i}\colon S^{(n)}\to S^{(n-1)}

called the
face maps
.

•

for each
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, a function:

s^{n}_{i}\colon S^{(n)}\to S^{(n+1)}

called
degeneracy maps
.

The face maps and degeneracy maps are required to satisfy the following relations:

(SS1)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n+1

d^{n}_{i}\circ d^{n+1}_{j}=d^{n}_{j-1}\circ d^{n}_{i};

(SS2)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n+1
,
n\geq 1

d^{n+1}_{i}\circ s^{n}_{j}=s^{n-1}_{j-1}\circ d^{n}_{i};

(SS3)

\forall 0\leq i<n

d^{(n+1)}_{i}\circ s^{n}_{i}=d^{{n+1}}_{i+1}\circ s^{n}_{i}=\Id;

(SS4)

\forall 0\leq j+1<i\leq n+1
,
n\geq 1

d^{n+1}_{i}\circ s^{n}_{j}=s^{n-1}_{j}\circ d^{n}_{i-1};

(SS5)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n+1
,

s^{n+1}_{j}\circ s^{n}_{i}=s^{n+1}_{i}\circ s^{n}_{j-1}.

Although there are many kinds of simplicial sets. The most important kind for our purposes arises from a groupoid. The simplicial set associated to a groupoid is called the nerve of the groupoid.

Since the structure of a groupoid is completely determined by its nerve, we may sometimes conflate the two objects.

Definition 2.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}_{0}
is a groupoid. The
nerve
of
\mathcal{G}
is the simplicial set
\{\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where we recall that:

\mathcal{G}^{(n)}:=\overbrace{\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.47897pt{s}\kern 8.071pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.47897pt{s}\kern 8.071pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.38383pt{s}\kern 5.6321pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.29495pt{s}\kern 4.54321pt{t}}}}\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.47897pt{s}\kern 8.071pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.47897pt{s}\kern 8.071pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.38383pt{s}\kern 5.6321pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.29495pt{s}\kern 4.54321pt{t}}}}\cdots\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.47897pt{s}\kern 8.071pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.47897pt{s}\kern 8.071pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.38383pt{s}\kern 5.6321pt{t}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{s}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.29495pt{s}\kern 4.54321pt{t}}}}\mathcal{G}_{1}}^{n\text{-times}}.

By convention, we let
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}=\mathcal{G}_{0}
be the space of objects.
The face maps are defined to be:

d^{n}_{i}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1}):=\begin{cases}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{2}),&i=0,\\
(g_{n},\ldots,g_{i+2},\ g_{i+1}\cdot g_{i}\ ,g_{i-1},\ldots g_{1}),&0<i<n,\\
(g_{n-1},\ldots,g_{1}),&i=n,\end{cases}

when
n\geq 2
. In the case
n=1
the face maps are just the target and source maps:

d^{1}_{0}=\mathbf{t}\qquad d^{1}_{1}=\mathbf{s}.

The degeneracy maps are:

s^{n}_{i}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1}):=(g_{n},\ldots,g_{i+1},(u\circ t)(g_{i}),g_{i},\ldots,g_{1})

where, in the above
u\colon M\to\mathcal{G}
is the unit embedding. By convention
s^{0}_{0}:=u
.
We leave it to the reader to verify that the axioms of simplicial sets hold.

In general, a simplicial object in a category consists of a collection of objects in that category, together with morphisms that satisfy axioms (SS1-5). However, in some cases it is reasonable to require some additional surjectivity assumptions. This is the case for simplicial manifolds:

Definition 2.3
.

A
simplicial manifold
is a simplicial set
M=\{M^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where each
M^{(n)}
is equipped with the structure of a smooth manifold and the face and degeneracy maps are smooth maps of manifolds. Finally, we require that the face maps are submersions.

The nerve of a Lie groupoid is an example of a simplicial manifold.

In the same vein, there is a concrete definition of a cosimplicial set in terms of sets and functions. We will write out the definition here for completeness but, as we see, the only distinction is reversing domain and codomain, as well as the order of composition.

Definition 2.4
.

A
cosimplicial set

S
consists of the following data:

•

a collection of sets indexed by non-negative natural numbers
\{S^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
;

•

for each
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, functions:

\phi^{n}_{i}\colon S^{(n-1)}\to S^{(n)}

called the
coface maps
;

•

for each
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, functions:

\sigma^{n}_{i}\colon S^{(n+1)}\to S^{(n)}

called
codegeneracy maps
.

This data is required to satisfy the following axioms:

(CS1)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n+1

\phi^{n+1}_{j}\circ\phi^{n}_{i}=\phi^{n+1}_{i}\circ\phi^{n}_{j-1},

(CS2)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n
,
n\geq 1

\sigma^{n}_{j}\circ\phi^{n+1}_{i}=\phi^{n}_{i}\circ\sigma^{n-1}_{j-1},

(CS3)

\forall 0\leq i<n

\sigma^{n}_{i}\circ\phi^{n+1}_{i}=\sigma^{n}_{i}\circ\phi^{n+1}_{i+1}=\Id,

(CS4)

\forall 0\leq j+1<i\leq n+1
,
n\geq 1

\sigma^{n}_{j}\circ\phi^{n+1}_{i}=\phi^{n}_{i-1}\circ\sigma^{n-1}_{j},

(CS5)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n+1
,

\sigma^{n+1}_{j}\circ\sigma^{n}_{i}=\sigma^{n+1}_{i}\circ\sigma^{n}_{j-1}.

2.3.
(Co)simplicial vector spaces

A particularly type of cosimplicial set relevant to our discussions are ones which are formed from vector spaces and linear maps. It is well known that cosimplicial vector spaces have a natural cohomology theory associated to them.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a fixed field.
A
(co)simplicial vector space
is a cosimplicial set
V=\{V^{(n)}\}
where each
V^{(n)}
is a vector space and the (co)face and (co)degeneracy maps are linear maps.

Throughout this article we will take the base field to be
\mathbb{R}
since that is the one that will be most relevant to us. However, cosimplicial vector spaces make sense over any field.

One important feature of (co)simplicial vector spaces is their simplicial (co)homology. This arises from what we will call the
simplicial differential
. We will define the “co” version of this object but the following definitions and proofs are essentially identical for the standard case.

Definition 2.6
.

Suppose
V
is a cosimplicial vector space. The
cochain complex
of
V
is the graded vector space:

\Ch(V):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V^{(n)}.

The
simplicial differential
on
V
is the degree one graded endomorphism

\delta\colon\Ch(V)\to\Ch(V)

with
n
-th homogeneous component given by:

\delta^{n}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{i}\phi^{n}_{i}\colon V^{(n)}\to V^{(n+1)}.

It is a classical fact that this differential squares to zero as a direct consequence of the face map relations. We include the calculation below for completeness.

Lemma 2.7
.

If
V
is a cosimplicial vector space and
\delta
is the simplicial differential, then
\delta^{2}=0
.

Proof.

We must show that

\forall n\geq 0,\qquad\delta^{n+1}\delta^{n}=0.

From the definition of a cosimplicial set, we know that:

(2.7.1)

\forall 0\leq i<j\leq n+1,\qquad\phi^{n}_{j}\phi^{n}_{i}=\phi^{n+1}_{i}\phi^{n}_{j-1}.

Using the definition of the simplicial differential observe that:

\delta^{n+1}\delta^{n}=\sum_{j=0}^{n+2}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(-1)^{i+j}\phi^{n+2}_{j}\phi^{n+1}_{i}

This sum can be split into two parts:

\delta^{n+1}\delta^{n}=\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq i\leq n+1}(-1)^{i+j}\phi^{n+2}_{j}\phi^{n+1}_{i}\right)+\left(\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n+2}(-1)^{i+j}\phi^{n+2}_{j}\phi^{n+1}_{i}\right)

Now if we apply Equation
2.7.1
to the second component of this sum we get:

\delta^{n+1}\delta^{n}=\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq i\leq n+1}(-1)^{i+j}\phi^{n+2}_{j}\phi^{n+1}_{i}\right)+\left(\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n+2}(-1)^{i+j}\phi^{n+2}_{i}\phi^{n+1}_{j-1}\right)

We can re-index the second sum to obtain:

\delta^{n+1}\delta^{n}=\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq i\leq n+1}(-1)^{i+j}\phi^{n+2}_{j}\phi^{n+1}_{i}\right)+\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq i\leq n+1}(-1)^{i+j+1}\phi^{n+2}_{j}\phi^{n+1}_{i}\right)

Since these two sums differ only by a sign, we conclude that
\delta^{n+1}\delta^{n}=0
.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

Let
V
be a cosimplicial vector space and let
\delta
be the associated simplicial differential. The
simplicial cohomology
, denoted
H^{\bullet}(V)
of
V
is defined to be the cohomology of the simplicial differential
H^{\bullet}(\Ch(V),\delta)
.

2.4.
Augmentation

A key notion that we will make use of is that of an augmentation. Traditionally, augmentations are ways of modifying bounded complexes by adding an additional term. They arise in the study of various kinds of resolutions in homological algebra. However, this notion has an analogue for simplicial structures and cosimplicial structures.

We will begin with defining augmentation in the context of simplicial manifolds.

Definition 2.9
.

Suppose
X
is a simplicial manifold. An
augmentation
of
X
consists of a smooth manifold
Y
together with a smooth map
p\colon X^{(0)}\to Y
satisfying
p\circ d_{1}=p\circ d_{0}
.

Augmentations of simplicial sets are sometimes thought of as extending the simplicial set by adding a set of “
-1
simplices.”

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Another perspective is to view an augmentation as giving a morphism of simplicial manifolds from
X
to
Y
where
Y
is thought of as a simplicial manifold where all face/degeneracy maps are the identity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Example 2.10
.

If
X
is the nerve of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
then one can think of an augmentation as a groupoid homomorphism
P\colon\mathcal{G}\to 1_{Y}
where
1_{Y}
is the identity groupoid over
Y
.

There is a corresponding definition for cosimplicial vector spaces.

Definition 2.11
.

Suppose
V
is a cosimplicial vector space. An
augmentation
of
V
consists of a vector space
W
together with a linear map
L\colon W\to V^{(0)}
with the property that
\phi_{1}L=\phi_{2}L

Just like with simplicial structures, augmentations of cosimplicial structures can be thought of as extensions of the simplicial structure of
V
or as a map from a standard vector space into a simplicial one.

Example 2.12
.

If
X
is a simplicial manifold then
C^{\infty}(X)
inherits the structure of a cosimplicial vector space. Furthermore, if
f\colon X\to Y
is an augmentation of
X
then the corresponding linear map
f^{*}\colon C^{\infty}(Y)\to C^{\infty}(X)
is an augmentation of
C^{\infty}(X)
.

Recall that one can construct a natural cochain complex out of any cosimplicial vector space. The notion of an augmentation carries over to this context as well.

Definition 2.13
.

Suppose
C
is a cochain complex. An
augmentation
of
C
consists of a linear map:

L\colon V\to C^{0}

with the property that
d^{0}L=0
.

If
L
is an augmentation of
C
then the
augmented complex

C_{L}
is the one obtained by extending
C
with a “-1” term.

C_{L}:=\left(\hbox to241.66pt{\vbox to17.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 120.82831pt\lower-8.72974pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{

An augmentation
L
is called
acyclic
if the augmented complex has trivial cohomology groups in all degrees.

The term ‘‘acyclic’’ here is not necessarily standard in the existing literature. We do not use the word ‘‘exact’’ because an exact augmentation usually refers to a weaker condition
6
6

6

Our understanding is that an
exact augmentation
is one where
H^{0}(C_{L})=0
. In other words, it is exact at degree zero.

An equivalent characterization of an augmentation is to define it as being morphism of cochain complexes of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

Acyclic augmentations are relevant due to the following observation:

Lemma 2.14
.

If
L
is an acyclic augmentation of
C
then
H^{i}(C)=0
for all
i\geq 1
and
L\colon V\to H^{0}(C)
is an isomorphism.

If we think of
L
as a morphism of complexes then the above lemma amounts to the observation: “acyclic augmentations are quasi-isomorphisms.” In the context of complexes this fact seems rather obvious. However, there is corresponding version of this fact for double and triple complexes which are less obvious and rather useful.

Augmentations of simplicial and cosimplicial structures directly correspond to augmentations of the associated cochain complexes.

Example 2.15
.

Suppose
V
is a cosimplicial vector space. Let
\Ch(V)
be the associated cochain complex. Then
L\colon W\to V
is an augmentation of
V
as a cosimplicial vector space if and only if
L
is an augmentation of
\Ch(V)
as a cochain complex.

2.5.
Groupoid cohomology

We saw that, associated to any cosimplicial vector space, there is an associated cochain complex and cohomology. It is worth paying particular attention to a special case where the cosimplicial vector space arises from a Lie groupoid.

The motivation behind studying this cohomology theory is that it gives a sort of model for the space of functions on the singular space (i.e. the stack) associated to
\mathcal{G}
. In fact, when a Lie groupoid is Morita equivalent to a smooth manifold, it turns out that the groupoid cohomology is isomorphic to the space of functions on the orbit space.

Definition 2.16
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid. For a natural number
n
the
cosimplicial algebra
of
\mathcal{G}
, written
\Ch(\mathcal{G})
, is defined so that:

\Ch(\mathcal{G})^{(n)}:=C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)}).

The coface and codegeneracy maps are the pullbacks along the face and degeneracy maps of the nerve:

\phi^{n}_{i}:=(d^{n}_{i})^{*}\qquad\sigma^{n}_{i}:=(s^{n}_{i})^{*}.

Definition 2.17
.

The
groupoid cochain complex
, also denoted
\Ch(\mathcal{G})
:

\Ch(\mathcal{G}):=\left(\hbox to208.66pt{\vbox to18.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 104.32632pt\lower-9.09918pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{

The differential here is just the simplicial differential for
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G})
. In this case it takes the form of an alternating sum of pullbacks along face maps:

\delta^{n}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{i}(d^{n+1}_{i})^{*}\colon C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)})\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}).

The cohomology:

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}):=H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})

is called the
groupoid cohomology
of
\mathcal{G}
.

2.6.
Double complexes

Double complexes will be a key component of our technical arguments. However, we will not need a full treatment of the topic. In particular, we will not make any (explicit) references to spectral sequences. The reason for this is that the actual properties of double complexes that we rely on will be fairly elementary.

Definition 2.18
.

A double (cochain) complex
consists of a bigraded vector space
D=\bigoplus\limits_{p,q\in\mathbb{N}}D^{p,q}
equipped with two bigraded linear endomorphisms:

d_{H}:D^{\bullet,\bullet}\to D^{\bullet,\bullet+1}\qquad d_{V}\colon D^{\bullet,\bullet}\to D^{\bullet+1,\bullet}

of degree
(0,1)
and
(1,0)
, respectively, satisfying

d_{H}^{2}=0,\qquad d_{V}^{2}=0,\qquad d_{H}d_{V}=d_{V}d_{H}.

Such a double complex can be illustrated by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{2,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{2,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{0,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{0,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}

There are many different kinds of cohomologies that can be constructed from a double complex. The most basic ones are the horizontal and vertical cohomologies. These are bigraded vector spaces constructed by applying the cohomology functor to one direction at a time.

Definition 2.19
.

The vertical cohomology

H_{V}(D)
of a double complex
D
is the one obtained by taking the cohomology of each column individually. The vertical cohomology is natural bigraded and:

H_{V}^{ij}(D):=H^{i}(D^{\bullet,j},d_{V}).

The horizontal cohomology is defined similarly:

H_{H}^{ij}(D):=H^{j}(D^{i\bullet},d_{H}).

Perhaps the most important cohomology of a double complex is the “total” cohomology.

Definition 2.20
.

Given a double complex
D^{\bullet,\bullet}
with horizontal and vertical differentials
d_{H}
and
d_{V}
, the total complex
\mathrm{Tot}(D^{\bullet,\bullet})
is a defined to be:

D_{\mathrm{tot}}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}D^{p,q},

with the differential
d_{\mathrm{tot}}:D_{\mathrm{tot}}^{\bullet}\to D_{\mathrm{tot}}^{\bullet+1}
given by:

d_{\mathrm{tot}}=d_{H}+(-1)^{p}d_{V}\quad\text{on}\quad D^{p,q}.

The total cohomology, simply written
H(D)
is the cohomology of the total complex.

The total cohomology is quasi-isomorphic to the diagonal cohomology, which is defined using the complex
\{D^{k,k}\}_{k\geq 0}
. As we will not need this in this article, we will not discuss the diagonal cohomology further. In general, computing the total cohomology of a double complex can be very complicated. Luckily for us, the kinds of double complexes that we will see in this article are comparatively simple. Our primary tool for computing total cohomology is the notion of an augmentation.

The augmentation of a double complex is a fairly natural generalization of the notion we defined for standard complexes. However, the two-dimensional nature of double complexes means that there are actually two natural notions of augmentation.

Definition 2.21
.

Suppose
D
is a double complex and
V
is a cochain complex. A
left augmentation

L\colon V\to D
consists of a morphism of cochain complexes:

L\colon V\to D^{\bullet,0}

with the property that
d_{H}L=0
.
A
bottom augmentation
is a morphism of cochain complexes:

B\colon W\to D^{0,\bullet}

with the property that
d_{H}B=0
.

Left and bottom augmentations can be illustrated by a diagram like so:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{2,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{2,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{0,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{0,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

A left augmentation is called
acyclic
if the augmented rows are all exact. In other words, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{n,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{n,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}

is exact. A bottom augmentation is called
acyclic
if the augmented columns are exact.

The following lemma is the main reason we care about augmentation of double complexes. It is a fairly standard fact in homological algebra by spectral sequence so we will not provide the proof here.

Lemma 2.22

(Augmentation lemma)
.

Suppose
D
is a double complex and suppose
L\colon V\to D
is an acyclic left augmentation of
D
.

Then
L
induces an isomorphism
L_{*}\colon H(V)\to H(D)
.

In other words, acyclic left/bottom augmentations are quasi-isomorphisms with respect to the total complex.

3.
Shifting (Co)simplicial structures (Decalages)

Simplicial sets come with a lot of inherited structure.
We will now highlight some operations one can apply to any simplicial set to construct new simplicial sets.
In the existing literature this operation is referred to as the “decalage” of a simplicial set.
In this article we will simply refer to the operation as the left/right shift of a simplicial set.

One can find more discussion of this topic in Illusie
[
Ill71
]
and but we will give a self contained explanation here.
Our philosophy will be to keep the terminology fairly grounded and proofs quite low tech.

3.1.
Left and right shifts of simplicial structures

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose
S
is a simplicial set with face maps
\{d^{n}_{i}\}
and degeneracy maps
s^{n}_{i}
. If
k\geq 0
is a natural number the
k
-th left shift of
S
is the simplicial set
S_{L}[k]
where:

S_{L}[k]^{(n)}:=S^{(k+n)}

and with face and degeneracy maps:

(d_{L}[k])^{n}_{i}:=d^{k+n}_{i},\qquad(s_{L}[k])^{n}_{i}:=s^{k+n}_{i}.

The
right shift
is denoted
S_{R}[k]
and also has:

S_{R}[k]^{(n)}:=S^{(k+n)}

but with face and degeneracy maps:

(d_{R}[k])^{n}_{i}:=d^{k+n}_{k+i},\qquad(s_{R}[k])^{n}_{i}:=s^{k+n}_{k+i}.

\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
d^{4}_{0}
d^{4}_{1}
d^{4}_{2}
d^{4}_{3}
d^{4}_{4}
d^{3}_{0}
d^{3}_{1}
d^{3}_{2}
d^{3}_{3}
d^{2}_{0}
d^{2}_{1}
d^{2}_{2}
d^{1}_{0}
d^{1}_{1}

Figure 1.
Face maps above the line are the ones included in the
S_{R}[2]

If we arrange the face maps of the simplicial set
S
into an infinite triangle, we can visualize the left and right shifts of
S
as picking out all elements above a particular node on the boundary. For example, in Figure
1
the second right shift is obtained by picking out all face maps above the face map
d^{2}_{2}
. Left and right shifts of cosimplicial vector spaces are defined similarly:

Definition 3.2
.

Suppose
V
is a cosimplicial vector space with coface maps
\{\phi^{n}_{i}\}
and degeneracy maps
\sigma^{n}_{i}
. If
k\geq 0
is a natural number the
k
-th left shift of
V
is the simplicial set
V_{L}[k]
where:

V_{L}[k]^{(n)}:=V^{(k+n)}

and with face and degeneracy maps:

(\phi_{L}[k])^{n}_{i}:=\phi^{k+n}_{i},\qquad(\sigma_{L}[k])^{n}_{i}:=\sigma^{k+n}_{i}.

The
right shift
is denoted
V_{R}[k]
and also has:

V_{R}[k]^{(n)}:=V^{(k+n)}

but with face and degeneracy maps:

(\phi_{R}[k])^{n}_{i}:=\phi^{k+n}_{k+i},\qquad(\sigma_{R}[k])^{n}_{i}:=\sigma^{k+n}_{k+i}.

3.2.
Shift double structures

Through the use of left and right shifts, one obtains a double simplicial structure from any simplicial set
S
. Since we are mainly interested in groupoids we will discuss this primarily in the context of Lie groupoids but shift double structures make sense for any simplicial object.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid. The
shift double groupoid
, denoted by
\mathcal{G}_{\shift}
of
\mathcal{G}
is the one obtained by arranging the left shifts of
\mathcal{G}
into the rows and the right shifts of
\mathcal{G}
into the columns as in the below:

Remark 3.4
.

The columns and rows of the shift double groupoid are the nerves of groupoid structures. They actually come from the natural action of
\mathcal{G}
on itself from the left and right, respectively. We usually think of the rows and columns as being indexed by natural numbers starting at
0
. Therefore,
0
-th column corresponds to the nerve of the (left) action groupoid
\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}
and the
n
-th column corresponds to the nerve of the action groupoid
\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}
.

The shift double of a cosimplicial object is defined in a similar way.

Definition 3.5
.

Given a cosimplicial vector space
V
the
shift double
of
V
, denoted
V_{\shift}
, is obtained by arranging the left and right shifts into the rows and columns (respectively) of a double structure as below:

V_{\shift}:=\left(\hbox to210.32pt{\vbox to142.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 105.15964pt\lower-71.45737pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-105.15964pt}{-71.45737pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

3.3.
Shift double complexes

If
V
is a cosimplicial vector space and
V_{\shift}
is its associated shift double, then each row and column of
V_{\shift}
is a cosimplicial vector space and has an associated cochain complex.

Definition 3.6
.

If
k
is a natural number then the
k
-th (left) shifted differential is defined to be the cosimplicial differential for the
k
th shifted complex
V_{L}[k]
. More concretely, for
n\geq 0
we have:

\delta_{L}[k]^{n}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{i}\phi^{k+n+1}_{i}\colon V^{(n+m)}\to V^{(n+m+1)}.

Similarly, the right shifted differential is:

\delta_{R}[k]^{n}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{i}\phi^{k+n+1}_{k+i}\colon V^{(n+m)}\to V^{(n+m+1)}.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
V
be a cosimplicial vector space. The
shift double complex
of
V
, denoted by
\Ch(V)_{\shift}
, is the one obtained by taking the horizontal maps to be the left shifts of the simplicial differential and vertical maps to be the right shifts of the simplicial differential as illustrated below:

\Ch(V)_{\shift}:=\left(\hbox to249.93pt{\vbox to178.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 126.77069pt\lower-89.45728pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

We think of this double complex as being defined for non-negative degree. When indexing this structure we take the first index to indicate the row and the second index to indicate the column so:

V_{\shift}^{(n,m)}:=V^{(n+m+1)}

Example 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid. Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the associated cosimplicial algebra of functions on the nerve of
\mathcal{G}
. Then
\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{\shift}
is the double complex obtained by applying the “groupoid cochain complex” functor to the shift double of
\mathcal{G}
. We will refer to this as the
shift double complex of
\mathcal{G}
.

4.
Retractions of (co)simplicial structures

Many of our arguments will rely on proving that a given left/bottom augmentation is acyclic. This will be primarily accomplished by constructing a homotopy operator that exhibits the fact that all cohomology groups vanish.

4.1.
Retractions along augmentations

In the simplicial context, the homotopy operators we will construct correspond to the notion of a retraction. Specifically, a retraction along an augmentation of the simplicial structure.

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
X
is a simplicial manifold and
p\colon X^{(0)}\to Y
is an augmentation. A
left retraction
of
X
along
P
consists of the following data:

A collection of smooth maps
\{r^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where
r^{0}\colon Y\to X^{(0)}
and for
n\geq 1
:

r^{n}\colon X^{(n-1)}\to X^{(n)}

and which satisfies the following properties:

•

r
intertwines
d^{1}_{1}
and
p
:

d^{1}_{1}\circ r^{1}=r^{0}\circ p,

•

for all
n\geq 1
:

d^{n}_{0}\circ r^{n}=\Id,

•

r
‘‘shifts’’ the face maps to the left
7
7

7

By shifting to the left we mean that it “decreases” the index
i
by one. We imagine the face maps
d^{n}_{i}
as being arranged in increasing order from left to right.
. For all
1\leq i\leq n+1
:

d^{n+1}_{i}\circ r^{n+1}=r^{n}\circ d^{n}_{i-1}.

Left retracts are actually homotopy equivalences between the original simplicial set
X
and
Y
thought of as a trivial simplicial set. The only difference from the standard theory of simplicial sets is that we are working in the smooth category and so we require that the maps involved are smooth.

We will not spend much time discussing general homotopy equivalences between simplicial sets formally, however, and we will keep our discussion grounded by speaking in terms of these explicit retractions.

4.2.
Examples of retractions

We are mainly interested in the nerve of a Lie groupoid. Therefore, our canonical example of a left retract of a Lie groupoid comes from a “principal” groupoid.

Definition 4.2
.

A Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is
principal
if the isotropy groups are trivial and the orbit space
B
admits a smooth structure which makes the projection
p:\mathcal{G}_{0}\to B
a submersion.

Principal groupoids appear frequently in the study of Lie groupoids. They have a few equivalent names and characterizations. We use the term principal because they are precisely the groupoids for which the natural action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}_{0}
constitutes a principal groupoid bundle.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a principal Lie groupoid. Suppose there exists a global section
\sigma\colon B\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
of
p
. Then there exists a left retract of
\mathcal{G}
to
B
along
p
.

Proof.

The proof is by a direct construction. Let us define a family of maps as follows:

r^{0}:=\sigma\colon B\to\mathcal{G}_{0}\

r^{1}\colon\mathcal{G}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}

defined uniquely by the equations:

\mathbf{t}\circ r^{1}=\Id,\qquad\mathbf{s}\circ r^{1}=r^{0}\circ p.

For
n\geq 2
we take:

r^{n}(g_{n-1},\ldots,g_{1}):=\left(g_{n-1},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n-1}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\cdot(r^{1}\circ t)(g_{n-1}))\right).

We claim that this data constitutes a left retract of
\mathcal{G}
to
B
along
p
.

First let us explain why
r^{1}
is well defined. Since the isotropy groups of
\mathcal{G}
are trivial, an arrow in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is completely determined by its source and target. Furthermore, since
r^{0}
is a section of
p
, for any point
x\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
there is a unique arrow in
\mathcal{G}
with source
r^{0}\circ p(x)
and target
x
. And the smoothness of the map
r^{0}
follows from the uniqueness of its choice.

It is not difficult to verify that
d^{n}_{0}\circ r^{n}=\Id
. For the case
n=1
we have this by construction of
r^{1}
. To show that this is a left retract we must show that for all
1\leq i\leq n
we have:

d^{n}_{i}\circ r^{n}=r^{n-1}\circ d^{n}_{i-1}.

The above equation is fairly straightforward to verify for
i<n
. To show the case
i=n
, it is helpful to first point out that the map
r^{1}
has the following property for all
x\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
h\in\mathcal{G}
with
\mathbf{s}(h)=x
:

h\cdot r^{1}(x)=r^{1}(t(h)).

With this fact in mind, we get the following computation for all
(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})\in\mathcal{G}^{(n-1)}
:

\displaystyle d^{n}_{n}\circ r^{n}(g_{n-1},\ldots,g_{1})=d^{n}_{n}

\displaystyle\left(g_{n-1},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n-1}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\cdot r^{1}(\mathbf{t}(g_{n-1}))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g_{n-2},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n-1}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\cdot r^{1}(\mathbf{t}(g_{n-1}))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g_{n-2},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n-2}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\cdot g_{n}^{-1}\cdot r^{1}(\mathbf{t}(g_{n}))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g_{n-2},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n-2}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\cdot r^{1}(\mathbf{t}(g_{n-2}))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{n-1}\circ d^{n-1}_{n-1}(g_{n-1},\ldots,g_{1}).

∎

Example 4.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid and let
k\geq 1
be a natural number. Consider the right shift
\mathcal{G}_{R}[k]
. Associated to this right shift, there is a natural projection map
d^{k}_{k}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(k)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(k-1)}
which can easily be seen to be invariant with respect to the source and target of
\mathcal{G}_{R}[k]
.

Furthermore, there is a natural left retract of
\mathcal{G}_{R}[k]
to
\mathcal{G}^{(k-1)}
along
d^{k}_{k}
given by:

\{s^{k+n-1}_{k}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(k+n-1)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(k+n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

That this data constitutes a left retract follows directly from the axioms regarding how face maps and degeneracy maps interact in a simplicial set. We call this the
standard retract
of
\mathcal{G}_{R}[k]
to
\mathcal{G}^{(k-1)}
.

4.3.
Left retracts of cosimplicial vector spaces

Let us now take a look at the left retracts in the context of cosimplicial vector spaces.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
V
be a cosimplicial vector space and suppose we are given an augmentation
L\colon W\to V^{(0)}
. A
left retract

\{\theta^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
V
along
L
consists of a family of linear maps
\{\theta^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where:

\theta^{0}\colon V^{(0)}\to W

and for
n\geq 1
we have:

\theta^{n}\colon V^{(n)}\to V^{(n-1)}

and which satisfies the following equations for all
n\geq 0
:

•

\theta
intertwines
\phi^{1}_{1}
and
L
:

\theta^{1}\phi^{1}_{1}=L\theta^{0},

•

for all
n\geq 1
,
\phi^{n}_{0}
is a left inverse of
\theta^{n}
:

\theta^{n}\phi^{n}_{0}=\Id,

•

\theta
“shifts” the face maps to the left. For all
1\leq i\leq n+1
:

\theta^{n+1}\phi^{n+1}_{i}=\phi^{n}_{i-1}\theta^{n}.

Example 4.6
.

The notion of a left retract for cosimplicial vector spaces is the dual to the notion of a left retract for Lie groupoids. Given an augmentation of a Lie groupoid
p\colon\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\to Y
and a left retraction
\{r_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
along
p
then
\{r_{n}^{*}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
constitutes a left retraction of
\Ch(\mathcal{G})
along
p^{*}\colon C^{\infty}(Y)\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(0)})
.

There is also a version of the standard left retract for shifted simplicial structures for cosimplicial vector spaces.

Example 4.7
.

Let
V
be a cosimplicial vector space and
k\geq 1
be a natural number. Consider the right shift
V_{R}[k]
. The linear map
\phi^{k}_{k}\colon V^{(k-1)}\to V^{(k)}
is invariant for
V_{R}[k]
. We can construct a left retract of
V_{R}[k]
to
V^{(k-1)}
along
\phi^{k}_{k}
by taking the following maps:

\{\sigma^{k+n-1}_{k}\colon V^{(k+n)}\to V^{(k+n-1)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

This construction is just the cosimplicial vector space analogue of Example
4.4
.

4.4.
Retractions and cohomology

Augmentations and left retracts of cosimplicial vector spaces have cohomological implications as well. Left retractions are useful because they can be used to prove that augmentations are acyclic.

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose
V
is a cosimplicial vector space and
L\colon W\to V
is an augmentation. If there exists a left retract
\{\theta_{n}\}
of
W
along
L
then
L
is acyclic.

In particular, for all
n\geq 1
we have that:

H^{n}(V)=0

and
L_{*}\colon W\to H^{0}(W)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Recall that an acyclic augmentation is one for which the augmented complex:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

has trivial cohomology groups in all degrees.

We will do this by showing that the
\theta
’s constitute a contracting homotopy of the augmented complex.

Using the definition of a left retract, a direct calculation (just a straightforward application of the definition) shows that we have the following three equations:

•

For
n\geq 1
we have:

\theta^{n+1}\delta^{n}+\delta^{n-1}\theta^{n}=\Id,

•

At level
n=0
we have:

\theta^{1}\delta^{0}-L\theta^{0}=\Id,

•

At level
n=-1
we have:

\theta^{0}L=\Id.

These three equations immediately imply that all cohomology groups of the augmented complex vanish.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a principal Lie groupoid and
p\colon\mathcal{G}_{0}\to B
be the projection to the orbit space. If
p
admits a global section, then the groupoid cohomology of
\mathcal{G}
is concentrated in degree zero and
p^{*}\colon C^{\infty}(B)\to H^{0}(\mathcal{G})
is an isomorphism.

Actually, the above corollary is true even if a global section does not exist. The idea is that, even when a global section does not exist at the level of spaces, one can still use local sections together with a partition of unity to construct a retraction at the level of groupoid cochains. We will see this argument made more explicitly later (See Section
8.2
).

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
V
be a cosimplicial vector space and let
V_{R}[k]
be a right shift. Then the map
\phi^{k}_{k}\colon V^{(k-1)}\to V^{(k)}
constitutes an acyclic augmentation of
V_{R}[k]
.

In particular, the cohomology of
V_{R}[k]
is concentrated in degree zero and the map
\phi^{k}_{k}\colon V^{(k-1)}\to H^{0}(V_{R}[k])
is an isomorphism.

4.5.
Cohomology of shift double structures

We can now compute the cohomology of shift double structures. The last observation of the previous subsection was that the cohomology of a shift of a cosimplicial vector space is concentrated in degree zero.

More specifically, every shift comes with a natural acyclic augmentation. We can put all of these augmentations together to construct acyclic augmentations of the full double structure.

If
V
is a cosimplicial vector space. There are two natural inclusions of the cochain complex of
V
into its shift double complex.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
V
be a cosimplicial vector space. The
standard left augmentation
, denoted

L_{V}\colon\Ch(V)\to\Ch(V)_{\shift}

is given by the face maps:

\left\{\phi^{n+1}_{0}\colon V^{(n)}\to V^{(n+1)}=V_{\shift}^{(n,0)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

Similarly, the
standard bottom augmentation
, denoted

B_{V}\colon\Ch(V)\to\Ch(V)_{\shift}

is given by the face maps:

\{\phi^{n+1}_{n+1}:V^{(n)}\to V^{(n+1)}=V_{\shift}^{(0,n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

These two maps are illustrated below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(4)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(5)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(4)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{3}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{2}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{1}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{L}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{R}[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{2}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{3}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

It turns out that these left and bottom augmentations are actually quasi-isomorphisms. This means that the shift double structure can be thought of as an “exploded” model for the original object.

Let us state our claim more clearly:

Lemma 4.12
.

The bottom augmentation

B_{V}\colon\Ch(V)\to\Ch(V)_{\shift}

and left augmentation

L_{V}\colon\Ch(V)\to\Ch(V)_{\shift}

are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

We prove this using the augmentation lemma. We saw earlier in Example
4.7
that one can construct a left retraction of the augmented complexes associated to each column. We also learned that this implies that the augmented rows are acyclic. By the Augmentation Lemma (Lemma
2.22
), it follows that
B_{V}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

A symmetrical argument can be applied to
L_{V}
. The only difference is that, technically, one must use “right retractions” rather than left which we have not formally defined or constructed. However, this is only a matter of adjusting the indexing in our definitions.
∎

5.
Target retractions over open sets

Briefly, a target retraction is a special kind of left retraction that takes place over an open subset
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
of the objects of a Lie groupoid.

Notice that gave explicit formulas for constructing left retractions of the right shifts of a Lie groupoid (See Example
4.4
) but, in general, there is no standard left retract for a left shift.

It turns out that it is still possible to construct left retracts of left shifts by choosing a section of the target map. Some complications in the following definitions come from the fact that it will be useful for us to be able to do this for an
arbitrary local section
of the target map defined over open subsets of the objects. Retractions constructed in this way we will call “target families.”

5.1.
Restricting left shifts to open sets

Note that the left shift
\mathcal{G}_{L}[1]
of a Lie groupoid is actually a principal groupoid with orbit space
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
. The projection to the orbit space is given by the target map:
t\colon\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.

Given an open subset
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
it makes sense to restrict the left shift to the open set
U
by restricting to all arrows that lie “above”
U
.

Towards that end, we have the following definition:

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid throughout this section. Given an open set
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{0}
we write
U^{(0)}:=U
and for
n\geq 1
:

U^{(n)}:=\{(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})\ :\ t(g_{n})\in U\}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(n)}

Remark 5.2
.

Warning: The nerve notation is suggestive here but the collection
\{U^{(n)}\}
does not quite constitute a simplicial submanifold of the nerve of
\mathcal{G}
. The reason for this is that not all of the face maps are defined for
U^{(n)}
. In general, the image of
d^{n}_{n}|_{U^{(n)}}
is not contained in
U^{(n-1)}
.

Although this collection does not constitute a simplicial submanifold of
\mathcal{G}
. We can use this family to construct subgroupoids of the shifts of
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let:

U[k]^{(n)}:=\left\{U^{(n+k)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

We equip
U[k]^{(\bullet)}
with the structure of a simplicial manifold by letting it inherit its face maps from
\mathcal{G}[k]
.
In other words we take:

d^{n}_{i}|_{U^{(n)}}\colon U^{(n)}\to U^{(n-1)}.

This is well defined because the only face map that does not restrict to the family
d^{n}_{i}\colon U^{(n)}\to U^{(n-1)}
is the “top” face map where
i=n
. Since any left shift of
\mathcal{G}
omits this face map, one obtains a well defined simplicial manifold structure
8
8

8

Actually the simplicial manifold
U[k]
is the nerve of a Lie groupoid. It is the nerve of the (right) action groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
on
U^{(k)}
. This groupoid is principal and has orbit space
U^{(k-1)}
.
on
U[k]
.

If
\{U_{a}\}_{a\in A}
is an open cover of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
then for each
k\geq 1
this construction provides us with an “simplicial” open cover
\{U_{a}[k]\}_{a\in A}
of
\mathcal{G}_{L}[k]
.

5.2.
Target families

Roughly speaking, a target family is a special kind of left retract of the local left shift
U[1]
where
U
is an open subset of
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 5.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid and we are given an open set
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{0}
together with a section
r^{0}\colon U\to U^{(1)}
of the target map
\mathbf{t}
.

The associated
target family over
U
is the collection of maps:

\left\{r^{n}\colon U^{(n)}\to U^{(n+1)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

where for all
n\geq 1
:

r^{n}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})\mapsto\left(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1},\,(g_{n}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\cdot r^{0}(t(g_{n}))\right).

Example 5.5
.

Consider the identity section
u\colon\mathcal{G}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}
. This section has an associated target family where
r^{0}=u
and for
n\geq 1
we have:

r^{n}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})=(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid and let
\{r^{n}\colon U^{(n)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a target family. Then we have that for all
n\geq 1
:

d^{n+1}_{i}\circ r^{n}=\begin{cases}\Id,&i=0,\\
r^{n-1}d_{i-1}^{n},&1\leq i\leq n.\end{cases}

The statement of the lemma is essentially the proof as it follows from a rather straightforward computation. It tells us that any target family defines a left retract of the associated subgroupoids of the left shifts of
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose we have a target family
\{r^{n}\}
over
U
. Then the collection
\{r^{k-1+n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
constitutes a left retract of the groupoid
U[k]
along the map
d^{k}_{0}\colon U[k]^{(0)}\to U^{(n-k)}
.

6.
Groupoid homomorphisms

6.1.
Weak equivalences

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
be Lie groupoids. A groupoid homomorphism
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
consists of a pair of smooth functions:

F_{1}\colon\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{1},\qquad F_{0}\colon\mathcal{H}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}

with the properties that
F(\mathbf{t}(h))=\mathbf{t}(F_{1}(h))
and
F_{1}(\mathbf{s}(h))=\mathbf{s}(F_{1}(h))
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
and:

\forall(h_{2},h_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{(2)},\qquad F_{1}(h_{2}h_{1})=F_{1}(h_{2})F_{1}(h_{1}).

Definition 6.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid. A smooth map
f\colon N\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
is
smoothly essentially surjective
if the smooth function:

\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{f}}}}N\to\mathcal{G}_{0},\qquad(g,x)\mapsto\mathbf{t}(g)

is a surjective submersion.

Given a function
f\colon N\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
which is smoothly essentially surjective the
pullback groupoid
is the Lie groupoid
f^{!}\mathcal{G}
where:

(f^{!}\mathcal{G})_{0}:=N,\qquad(f^{!}\mathcal{G})_{1}:=N\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{f}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-11.1085pt{f}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{f}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-11.1085pt{f}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{f}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.8335pt{f}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{f}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.74461pt{f}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{f}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{f}}}}N.

The source and target maps are:

\mathbf{s}(x,g,y):=y,\qquad\mathbf{t}(x,g,y):=x,

and the groupoid multiplication is given by:

(x,g,y)\cdot(y,h,z):=(x,gh,z).

Definition 6.3
.

A groupoid homomorphism
F
is called a
weak equivalence
if
F_{0}
is smoothly essentially surjective and the natural map:

F_{!}\colon\mathcal{H}\to f^{!}\mathcal{G},\qquad h\mapsto(\mathbf{t}(h),F_{1}(h),\mathbf{s}(h))

defines an isomorphism of Lie groupoids.

6.2.
Double groupoid of a groupoid homomorphism

Definition 6.4
.

Given a groupoid homomorphism
F
one can construct a double simplicial structure
\DG_{F}
which we call the
F
-double groupoid
:

In other words, we have

\DG_{F}^{(n,m)}:=\mathcal{G}^{(n+1,m)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, each column
\DG_{F}^{(\bullet,m)}
is the nerve of the (left) action groupoid:

\mathcal{G}\ltimes(\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}_{1}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}

g_{2}\cdot(g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1}):=(g_{2}g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1}).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, each column
\DG_{F}^{(n,\bullet)}
is the nerve of the
right
action groupoid:

(\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}_{0})\rtimes\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}_{0}

(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},x)\cdot h:=(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1}F_{1}(h),\mathbf{s}(h)).

The homomorphism
F
also defines natural maps from the
F
-double groupoid
\DG_{F}
into the shift double groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}
:

F_{{\shift}}\colon\DG_{F}\to\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}},

F_{{\shift}}^{(n,m)}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(n+1,m)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(n+m+1)},

F_{{\shift}}^{(n,m)}(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1}):=(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},F_{1}(h_{m}),\ldots,F_{1}(h_{1})).

6.3.
Double complex of a groupoid homomorphism

We saw that any Lie groupoid homomorphism has an associated double simplicial structure. Of course there is also a double complex.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid homomorphism. Write

\DC_{F}^{(n,m)}:=C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}).

Using the simplicial differentials for each row and column of the
F
-double groupoid, we obtain a double complex
\DC_{F}
which we call the
F
-double complex
.
