\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

The
F
-double complex is related to the groupoid cohomology of
\mathcal{G}
as well as the groupoid cohomology of
\mathcal{H}
. We begin by describing the relationship to the groupoid cohomology of
\mathcal{H}
.

There is a natural groupoid homomorphism
P_{F}
from the bottom row of the
F
-double groupoid to the nerve of
\mathcal{H}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{(2)}}

where:

(6.5.1)

P_{F}^{(n)}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}\to\mathcal{H}^{(n)}\qquad(g,h_{n},\ldots,h_{1})\mapsto(h_{n},\ldots,h_{1}).

This groupoid homomorphism induces a bottom augmentation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DC_{F}^{(0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DC_{F}^{(0,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DC_{F}^{(0,2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{H})^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{H})^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{H})^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

In particular, this means that there is a natural morphism in cohomology from
H(\mathcal{H})
to
H(\DC_{F})
. In fact, this is an isomorphism (a fact which we will prove shortly).

One can also relate the cohomology of
\mathcal{G}
to the cohomology of
\DC_{F}
in the following way: recall that there is a morphism of double groupoids from the
F
-double groupoid to the shift double groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
given by the maps:

F_{{\shift}}^{(n,m)}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(n+m+1)}

(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})\mapsto(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},F_{1}(h_{m}),\ldots,F_{1}(h_{1})).

This induces a morphism of double complexes:

F_{{\shift}}^{*}\colon\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{{\shift}}\to\DC_{F}.

On the other hand, there are also the standard bottom and left augmentations:

B^{*}\colon\Ch(\mathcal{G})\to\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{{\shift}},\qquad L^{*}\colon\Ch(\mathcal{G})\to\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{{\shift}}

which we know are quasi-isomorphisms.

We will conclude this section with a lemma that summarizes the relationships between the various groupoid cohomologies and double complexes.

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid homomorphism. Then we have a commutative diagram of simplicial structures:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DG_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

where
L
and
B
are the standard left and bottom augmentations and
P_{F}
is the map defined in Equation
6.5.1
.

The morphisms
L
,
B
and
P_{F}
admit left retractions. Therefore, the associated augmentations of complexes
L^{*},B^{*}
and
P_{F}^{*}
are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

Lemma
4.12
says that
L^{*}
and
B^{*}
are quasi-isomorphisms since they are the standard left and bottom augmentations of
\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{\shift}
. The only part of this lemma that has not already been proved is the claim that
P_{F}
admits a left retraction.

Let us fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We will construct a left retract of the
m
-column of
\DG_{F}
to
\mathcal{H}^{(m)}
along
P_{F}
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
r^{n}
be defined as follows:

r^{0}\colon\mathcal{H}^{(m)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}

(h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})\to(\mathbf{u}\circ F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}(h_{m}),h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})

and for
n\geq 1
:

r^{n}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}

(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})\mapsto(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1},\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{s}(g_{1}),h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})

To conclude that this is a left retract we must show that:

•

compatibility between
r
and
p
:

d^{1}_{1}\circ r^{1}=r^{0}\circ p;

•

for all
n\geq 1
:

d^{n}_{0}\circ r^{n}=\Id;

•

for all
1\leq i\leq n+1
:

d^{n+1}_{i}\circ r^{n+1}=r^{n}\circ d^{n}_{i-1},

where the maps
\{d^{n}_{i}\}
are the face maps for the
m
-column of
\DG_{F}
. However, these face maps are precisely the same as the face maps inherited from the
\mathcal{G}
-part of
\DG_{F}
.

For example, computing the LHS of the equation from the first bullet point gives:

\displaystyle d^{1}_{1}\circ r^{1}(g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1}))

\displaystyle=d^{1}_{1}(g_{1},\mathbf{u}\circ F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}(h_{m}),h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{u}\circ F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}(h_{m}),h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})

\displaystyle=r^{0}\circ p(g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})

The second bullet point will hold as a consequence of the right unit axiom. The third bullet point will hold as a consequence of the fact that these fact maps do not “touch” the inserted unit. We leave it to the reader to verify the details of these equations (it is just a direct calculation from the definition).
∎

7.
Cosimplicial Modules over groupoids

7.1.
Sheaves of modules

Suppose
M
is a smooth manifold. We write
C^{\infty}_{M}
to denote the sheaf of functions on
M
.

Definition 7.1
.

A sheaf of modules
\mathcal{E}
on a manifold
M
consists of a sheaf of
C^{\infty}_{M}
-modules
\mathcal{E}
. Given a smooth map
f\colon N\to M
the
pullback

f^{*}\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{E}
along
f
is sheaf:

U\mapsto\mathcal{E}(M)\underset{C^{\infty}_{M}(M)}{\otimes}C^{\infty}_{N}(U)

where
C^{\infty}_{M}(M)
acts on
C^{\infty}_{N}(U)
along the homomorphism
\alpha\mapsto f^{*}\alpha|_{U}
and we take the tensor product
\otimes
to be taken over
locally
finite sums/
In other words, elements of
f^{*}\mathcal{E}(U)
are represented by formal sums of the form:

\sum_{a\in A}e_{a}\otimes\rho_{a}

where
\rho_{a}
is a locally finitely supported family of functions on
U
.

Remark 7.2
.

Our definition of the pullback implicitly takes advantage of some particularly nice properties of sheaves of modules on manifolds. A standard argument using partitions of unity verifies that the pullback operation we have defined indeed results in a sheaf.

Example 7.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a sheaf of modules on a manifold
M
and let
\iota\colon U\to M
be the inclusion of an open subset. Then the pullback sheaf
\mathcal{E}|_{U}:=\iota^{*}U
has global sections
\iota^{*}\mathcal{E}(U)=\mathcal{E}(U)
.

Example 7.4
.

Suppose
E\to M
is a vector bundle. Then
\Gamma_{E}
the sheaf of sections of
E
is a sheaf of modules on
M
. If
f\colon N\to M
is a smooth map then
f^{*}\Gamma_{E}
is canonically isomorphic to
\Gamma_{f^{*}E}
where
f^{*}E=E\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi,f}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-15.26274pt{\pi,f}\kern 8.071pt{N}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi,f}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-15.26274pt{\pi,f}\kern 8.071pt{N}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi,f}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.8008pt{\pi,f}\kern 5.6321pt{N}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi,f}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-9.71191pt{\pi,f}\kern 4.54321pt{N}}}}\to N
is the usual pullback bundle.

Definition 7.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a sheaf of modules on
M
and
\mathcal{W}
is a sheaf of modules on
N
. A
morphism
from
(F,f)\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{W}
consists of a smooth map
f\colon M\to N
and a morphism of
C^{\infty}_{M}
-modules:

F\colon\mathcal{E}\to f^{*}\mathcal{W}.

A
comorphism

(F,f)\colon\mathcal{W}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}
consists of a smooth map
f\colon M\to N
and a morphism of
C^{\infty}_{M}
-modules:

F\colon f^{*}\mathcal{W}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}.

Example 7.6
.

Suppose
E\to M
and
W\to N
are vector bundles. If
F\colon E\to W
is a vector bundle map over
f\colon M\to N
. One can factor a bundle map through the pullback bundle by defining a pushforward operation:

F_{*}:E\to f^{*}W

There is a canonical isomorphism between
\Gamma_{f^{*}W}
and the pullback sheaf of modules
f^{*}\Gamma_{W}
. Hence, this yields a morphism of sheaves of modules

(F_{*},f)\colon\Gamma_{E}\to\Gamma_{W}

On the other hand, if we are given a smooth map
f\colon M\to N
and a morphism of vector bundles
G\colon f^{*}W\to E
covering the identity, then one obtains a natural comorphism of modules
E\to M

(G^{*},f)\colon\Gamma_{W}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\Gamma_{E}

Example 7.7
.

Suppose
f\colon M\to N
is a smooth map. The sheaves of
k
-forms
\Omega^{k}_{M}
and
\Omega^{k}_{N}
are sheaves of modules on
M
and
N
. The usual pullback
(f^{*},f)\colon\Omega^{k}_{M}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{k}_{N}
is a comorphism.

7.2.
Good cosimplicial modules

Not all cosimplicial modules will be well behaved with respect to weak equivalences. We will focus on a subset of them which we call “good” cosimplicial modules.

The property of being good can be thought of as a kind of Kan condition for the associated cosimplicial vector space. It’s slightly stronger than the usual Kan conditions however and one can roughly think of it as saying: “Kan filling maps of the groupoid can be lifted to Kan filling maps of the cosimplicial module in a way compatible with face maps.”

Definition 7.8
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid. A
cosimplicial module
on
\mathcal{G}
is a cosimplicial object in the category of modules with comorphisms. In other words, it consists of the following data:

•

for each natural number
n\geq 0
, a sheaf of modules
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
on
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
.
,

•

for each face map
d^{n}_{i}
, a comorphism of modules

(\phi^{n}_{i},d^{n}_{i})\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n-1)}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}^{(n)},

•

for each degeneracy map
s^{n}_{i}
, a comorphism of modules

(\sigma^{n}_{i},s^{n}_{i})\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n+1)}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}^{(n)}.

(Goodness) A cosimplicial module is
good
if given any local section
r^{0}\colon U\to\mathcal{G}_{1}
of the target map with associated target family
\{r^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a family of comorphisms

\left\{((r^{n})^{\#},r^{n})\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n+1)}|_{U^{(n+1)}}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}^{(n)}|_{U^{(n)}}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

which satisfies the target family relations:

(r^{n})^{\#}\phi^{n+1}_{i}=\begin{cases}\Id,&i=0,\\
\phi^{n}_{i-1}(r^{n-1})^{\#},&1\leq i\leq n.\end{cases}

At this time we are not aware of a natural example of a cosimplicial module over a groupoid which is not “good.” Any module which is sufficiently functorial is easily seen to be good.

Example 7.9
.

Suppose
M
is a smooth manifold, thought of as a trivial groupoid
M\rightrightarrows M
. Then the face and degeneracy maps are all just the identity. In this case, any cosimplicial module on
M\rightrightarrows M
will be good.

Example 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid and let
k\geq 0
be an integer. Consider the sheaves of differential forms on the nerve
\{\Omega_{\mathcal{G}^{(n)}}^{k}\}
then we we have coface maps:

((d^{n}_{i})^{*},d^{n}_{i})\colon\Omega_{\mathcal{G}^{(n-1)}}^{k}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\Omega_{\mathcal{G}^{(n)}}^{k}

which are the coface maps for a cosimplicial structure.

It is also clearly good since the functorial nature of this sheaf means any target family can certainly be lifted to the level of modules.

Example 7.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid and suppose we have a vector bundle
\pi\colon E\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
together with a (right) representation of
\mathcal{G}
on
E
.

One can construct a cosimplicial module by considering the action groupoid
E\rtimes\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows E
. Note that each level of the nerve of the action groupoid is of the form
E\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.7383pt{\pi}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.64941pt{\pi}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
which is a vector bundle over
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
. The face maps of the nerve of
\mathcal{G}\ltimes E\rightrightarrows E
are fiber-wise isomorphisms so they can be equivalently thought of as morphisms or comorphisms. Therefore, if we take:

\mathcal{E}^{(n)}:=\Gamma(E\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.7383pt{\pi}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.64941pt{\pi}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}^{(n)})

for each face map
d^{n}_{i}
of
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
one has a pullback map:

\phi^{n}_{i}\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n-1)}\to\mathcal{E}^{(n)}.

Hence we obtain a cosimplicial module over
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 7.12
.

The simplicial module in Example
7.11
is good.

Proof.

Suppose
r^{0}\colon U\to\mathcal{G}_{1}
is a section of the target map and
\{r^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is the associated target family. Now consider the bundle maps:

R^{n}\colon E\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.7383pt{\pi}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.64941pt{\pi}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}U^{(n)}\to E\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.7383pt{\pi}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.64941pt{\pi}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}U^{(n+1)},\qquad R^{n}(e,g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})):=(e,r^{n}(\pi(e))).

These fiberwise linear maps cover the target family on
\mathcal{G}
and one sees easily that they define a target family of the groupoid
E\rtimes\mathcal{G}
over
E|_{U}
. Since they are fiberwise isomorphisms, they define pullbacks at the level of sections and we define
(r^{n})^{\#}\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n+1)}\to\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
to be these pullback maps.
∎

7.3.
Cohomology of cosimplicial modules

Since the global sections of a cosimplicial module constitute a cosimplicial vector space. They have a natural cohomology theory.

Definition 7.13
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a cosimplicial module on
\mathcal{G}
. The global sections of
\mathcal{E}
form a cosimplicial vector space. Then the
cochain complex
of
\mathcal{E}
is defined to be the natural cochain complex associated to this cosimplicial vector space. More explicitly,
\Ch(\mathcal{E})
is the graded vector space:

\Ch(\mathcal{E}):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)})

equipped with the degree one graded map

\delta\colon\Ch(\mathcal{E})\to\Ch(\mathcal{E})

\delta^{n}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{i}\phi^{n}_{i}

7.4.
Pullbacks of cosimplicial modules

Definition 7.14
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid and let
\mathcal{E}
be a cosimplicial module on
\mathcal{G}
. If
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a groupoid homomorphism then we obtain a pullback
F^{*}\colon\Ch(\mathcal{G})\to\Ch(\mathcal{H})
at the level of groupoid cochains.

We define the pullback
F^{*}\mathcal{E}
to be the cosimplicial module on
\mathcal{H}
given by the pullbacks along the natural maps at the level of the nerves:

(F^{*}\mathcal{E})^{(n)}:=\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\underset{C^{\infty}_{\mathcal{G}^{(n)}}}{\otimes}C^{\infty}_{\mathcal{H}^{(n)}}.

The coface and codegeneracies are:

(F^{*}\phi^{n}_{i}):=(\phi^{n}_{i})\otimes(d^{n}_{i})^{*},\qquad(F^{*}\sigma^{n}_{i}):=(\sigma^{n}_{i})\otimes(s^{n}_{i})^{*},

where, in the above
d^{n}_{i}
and
s^{n}_{i}
, refer to the face and degeneracy maps for
\mathcal{H}
.

Given such a pullback, at each level
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the map:

(F^{n})^{\#}\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\to(F^{*}\mathcal{E})^{(n)}\qquad e\mapsto e\otimes 1

This induces a morphism at the level of chain complexes:

F^{\#}\colon\Ch(\mathcal{E})\to\Ch(F^{*}\mathcal{W}).

Example 7.15
.

If
E\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
is a right representation of
\mathcal{G}
then the pullback of the associated cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}
along a groupoid homomorphism
F
is just the sections of the pullback bundles:

F^{*}\left(E\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.97523pt{\pi}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.7383pt{\pi}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\pi}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.64941pt{\pi}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\right)\to\mathcal{H}^{(n)}

8.
Morita invariance

In this section we will prove our main theorems which demonstrate Morita invariance in a variety of contexts for cosimplicial modules. The key to all of our proofs is the ability to construct global left retractions out of local ones so that is what we will show in the first subsection.

8.1.
Left retracts of cosimplicial modules

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a cosimplicial module on
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\mathcal{W}
be a module over a manifold
B
and suppose we have a comorphism
(L,p)\colon\mathcal{W}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}
where
p\colon\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\to B
and
L\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
are augmentations of their respective structures.

A
left retract
of
\mathcal{E}
to
\mathcal{W}
along
(L,p)
consists of a left retract
\{r^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
along
p
together with a left retract
\{\theta^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the cosimplicial vector space
\mathcal{E}
to
\mathcal{W}
along
L
such that the combined pairs constitute module comorphisms. In other words, we have module comorphisms:

(\theta^{0},r^{0})\colon\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{W}

and for
n\geq 1

(\theta^{n},r^{n})\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}^{(n-1)}

which satisfy the axioms for a left retract along
(L,p)
.

Since a left retract in the category of module comorphisms constitutes a left retract in the category of vector spaces (when thinking in terms of global sections) it follows that the existence of such a retract implies that the augmentation
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
is acyclic at the level of global sections.

Unfortunately, it is a too optimistic to hope to construct global left retractions. Even when
L
is acyclic, it may be the case that no left retract exists inside of the category of module comorphisms. The basic problem is that surjective submersions do not admit global sections.

However, it turns out that if one can construct a left retraction locally (at the level of comorphisms) then it is actually possible to construct a global left retract (at the level of
vector spaces
). The trick is to take advantage of partitions of unity to piece together local retractions into global retractions.

To understand how this works, let us first suppose we have a cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{G}
, a module
\mathcal{W}
on a manifold
B
together with an augmentation
(L,p)\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
. If
\{U_{a}\}_{a\in A}
is an open cover of
B
then, for each
a\in A
, one can define restricted groupoids
\mathcal{G}_{a}
where the objects are

(\mathcal{G}_{a})_{0}:=p^{-1}(U_{a})

and the arrows are

(\mathcal{G}_{a})_{1}:=\mathbf{t}^{-1}(p^{-1}(U_{a}))=\mathbf{s}^{-1}(p^{-1}(U_{a})).

We can also restrict the modules to this cover:

\mathcal{W}_{a}:=\mathcal{W}|_{U_{a}}\qquad\mathcal{E}_{a}^{(n)}:=\mathcal{G}_{a}^{(n)}

One also obtains a restricted invariant comorphism:
(L_{a},p_{a})\colon\mathcal{W}_{a}\to\mathcal{E}_{a}
defined in the obvious way.

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose we have a module
\mathcal{W}
over a manifold
B
and a cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{G}
. Suppose we have a comorphism
(L,p)\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
where
\mathbf{t}\circ p=\mathbf{s}\circ p
. Suppose further that there exists an open cover
\{U_{a}\}_{a\in A}
of
B
such that for all
a\in A
, there exists a left retract:

\{(\theta^{n}_{a},r^{n}_{a})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

of the cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}_{a}
along
(L_{a},p_{a})
. Then there exists a left retract (of a cosimplicial vector space) of
\mathcal{E}
along
L
. Hence, the augmentation
L\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
is acyclic.

Proof.

Let
\rho_{a}
be a locally finite partition of unity subordinate to
\{U_{a}\}_{a\in A}
. Since
\mathbf{t}\circ p=\mathbf{s}\circ p
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a partition of unity
\{\rho^{n}_{a}\}
on
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
subordinate to the open cover
\{\mathcal{G}_{a}^{(n)}\}_{a\in A}
. This partition of unity is compatible with face maps in the sense that:

\forall a\in A,\ 1\leq i\leq n,\ e\in\mathcal{E}^{(n-1)},\qquad\phi^{n}_{i}(\rho^{n-1}_{a}\cdot e)=\rho^{n}_{a}\cdot\phi^{n}_{i}(e).

In this way, we can use the partition of unity to construct a global left retract (of cosimplicial vector spaces):

\theta^{n}:=\sum_{a\in A}\rho_{a}^{(n)}\theta^{n}_{a}

where on the right hand side we have implicitly taken advantage of the “extension maps”
\mathcal{E}_{a}\to\mathcal{E}
given by
e\mapsto\rho_{a}e
.

Since the partitions of unity are all compatible with the cosimplicial structure, it follows that
\theta^{n}
is a left retract of cosimplicial vector spaces along
L
.
∎

8.2.
Morita invariance of groupoid cohomology

Before we prove Morita invariance in the more general context, it is useful to explain the proof the “base case” of groupoid cohomology.

The following result is well known, c.f.
[
Cra03
]
, however, we will make reference to the proof of this theorem multiple times later.

Theorem 8.3
.

Suppose
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence. Then
F^{*}\colon\Ch(\mathcal{G})\to\Ch(\mathcal{H})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

It bears recalling the components of this diagram:

•

The maps
L
and
B
are the standard left and bottom augmentations which map
\Ch(\mathcal{G})
into the shift double complex
\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{{\shift}}
. We showed in Lemma
4.12
that they are quasi-isomorphisms.

•

The map
F_{{\shift}}^{*}
is the pullback along the smooth double groupoid homomorphism:

F_{{\shift}}\colon\DG_{F}\to\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}

from the
F
-double groupoid to the shift double groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
.

•

The map
F^{*}
is just the nerve-wise pullback map along
F
.

•

The arrow
P_{F}^{*}
is the one which appeared in Lemma
6.6
. It arises from the natural projections:

P_{F}^{(m)}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}\to\mathcal{H}^{(m)}.

In Lemma
6.6
, we proved that
P_{F}^{*}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

To prove the theorem, it suffices to show that
F^{*}_{\shift}\circ L
is a quasi-isomorphism. This is what we will now focus on demonstrating.

Let us fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let us consider the
n
-th row of
\DG_{F}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

By composing projection to the first component with the first face map
d_{0}
we get a submersion:

p\colon\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(n)},\qquad p(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},x)=(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{2}).

If we unpack the definition, we see that, at level
n
, the map
F^{*}_{\shift}\circ L
is just the pullback along
p
:

F^{*}_{\shift}\circ L=p^{*}\colon C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)})\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}).

The pullback
p^{*}
defines a left augmentation of the double complex
\DC_{F}
.
By Lemma
2.22
, if we can show this augmentation is acyclic then the proof is finished. We will show it is acyclic by constructing a left retract of each
n
column of
\DC_{F}
along
p^{*}
.

Now, according to Lemma
8.2
, we only need to construct a left retract locally since we can put them together using a partition of unity. Notice that
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
can be covered by open subsets of the form
U^{(n)}
where
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{0}
. Hence, it suffices to construct a retract over an open subset of this form.

Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
be an arbitrary open subset for which the map:

\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(0)},\qquad(g,x)\mapsto\mathbf{t}(g)

admits a section.

Such a section would take the form of a function:

(\sigma,\overline{f})\colon U\to\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}_{0},\qquad x\mapsto(\sigma(x),\overline{f}(x)),

where
\sigma\colon U\to U^{(1)}
is a section of the target map and
\overline{f}\colon U\to\mathcal{H}^{(0)}
is a smooth function which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}

Notice that if we restrict the
n
th-row of
\DG_{F}
to
p^{-1}(U^{(n)})
the resulting groupoid is of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

We will now construct a left retract
\{\rho^{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
for this groupoid. The idea behind the construction is, essentially, to “lift” the target family associated to
\sigma
to the rows of
\DG_{F}
.

Given
m\geq 1
, let
\rho^{m}
be defined as follows:

\rho^{m}\colon U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m-1)}\to U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)},

\rho^{m}(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1})=(g_{n+1},\ldots,g_{1},h_{m},\ldots,h_{1},\overline{h})

where
\overline{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{(1)}
is the unique element which satisfies:

F(\overline{h})=(g_{n+1}\cdots g_{1}\cdot F(h_{m})\cdots F(h_{1}))^{-1}r^{0}(t(g_{n+1})))

and

\mathbf{t}(h)=\mathbf{s}(h_{1})\qquad\mathbf{s}(\overline{h})=\overline{f}(\mathbf{t}(g_{n+1}))

This map is well defined since
F
is a weak equivalence (elements in
\mathcal{H}^{(1)}
are uniquely determined by their source, target, and image in
\mathcal{G}
).
For the case
m=0
we define:

\rho^{0}\colon U^{(n)}\to U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}

\rho^{0}(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1})=(g_{n},\ldots,g_{1},(g_{n}\cdots g_{1})^{-1}\sigma(\mathbf{t}(g_{n})),\overline{f}(\mathbf{t}(g_{n})))

A straightforward computation shows that
\{\rho^{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
constitutes a left retract along
p
. At the level of groupoid cochains we get a left retract
\{(\rho^{m})^{*}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of the cosimplicial vector space
C^{\infty}(\DG^{(n,\bullet)})
which implies that the augmented row
\DC^{(n,\bullet)}
is acyclic.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Notice that, in the proof of Theorem
8.3
, the construction of the retraction
\rho
is done in a way that makes it compatible with the target family induced by
\sigma
. To be precise, if
\{r^{n}\colon U^{(n)}\to U^{(n+1)}\}
is the target family associated to
\sigma
then we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n+1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n+m+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}^{(m-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{(n+m)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\shift}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\shift}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{n+m}}

The existence of this lift is a consequence of the assumption that
F
is a weak equivalence.

8.3.
Morita Invariance for sheaves on the big site

Definition 8.5
.

A
big site sheaf of modules

\mathcal{E}
is a sheaf on the site of smooth manifolds with values in module comorphisms. If
\mathcal{E}
is a big site sheaf of modules and
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid, then we get a cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G})
by applying
\mathcal{E}
to the entire nerve of
\mathcal{G}
.

Note that any cosimplicial module arising from a sheaf on the big site will be automatically a good cosimplicial module.

Example 8.6
.

If we fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The sheaf of differential
k
-forms
\Omega^{k}
is a big site sheaf. If
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie groupoid then
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{G})
will be the cosimplicial vector space of
k
-forms on
\mathcal{G}
. If
k=0
then this is just the usual cosimplicial module of functions on
\mathcal{G}
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
be Lie groupoids and let
\mathcal{E}
be a big site sheaf of modules. Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}
be the associated cosimplicial sheaves of modules. If
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence then
\mathcal{E}_{F}\colon\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof is essentially obtained by applying the functor
\mathcal{E}
to the constructions in the proof of Theorem
8.3
.

Recall that in the proof of Theorem
8.3
we considered the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DG_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

and observed/proved that
L
,
B
and
P_{F}
all admit retractions. Furthermore, we showed that
L\circ F_{\shift}
admits retractions locally.

Now we can apply the functor
\mathcal{E}
to the above diagram to obtain a new diagram:

\hbox to178.69pt{\vbox to65.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 89.26096pt\lower-27.89166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Furthermore, we can apply the functor to the constructions of the left retracts for the original diagram to obtain left retracts for
\mathcal{E}_{L}
,
\mathcal{E}_{B}
and
\mathcal{E}_{P_{F}}
. We also obtain local left retracts for
\mathcal{E}_{F_{\shift}}\mathcal{E}_{L}
. In Lemma
8.2
we observed that existence of local retracts suffices to conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{F_{\shift}}\mathcal{E}_{L}
is acyclic.

Therefore, all of the augmentations appearing in the above diagram are acyclic. Hence, they are quasi-isomorphisms. It follows that
\mathcal{E}_{F_{\shift}}
is a quasi-isomorphism and therefore
\mathcal{E}_{F}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 8.8
.

Let
k
be a positive integer and
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{H}
be Lie groupoids. If
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence then
F^{*}\colon\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{G})\to\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{H})
is a quasi-isomorphism of cosimplicial modules.

8.4.
Morita invariance for pullbacks

In this section, let us fix a groupoid homomorphism
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
. Suppose we are given a cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{G}
. Notice that there is a natural morphism of cosimplicial vector spaces

F^{\#}:=\Id\otimes 1\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B},\qquad e\mapsto e\otimes 1.

Hence, one obtains a morphism of the associated chain complexes. Our main theorem says that this morphism is a quasi-isomorphism when
F
is a weak equivalence and when
\mathcal{E}
is good.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
be a weak equivalence of groupoids and suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is a good cosimplicial module on
\mathcal{G}
. Then the pullback

F^{\#}\colon\mathcal{E}\to F^{*}\mathcal{E}

defines an isomorphism in cohomology.

Proof.

Recall that by Lemma
6.6
, that we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DG_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

where
L
,
B
and
P_{F}
are all augmentations that admit left retractions.

At the level of groupoid cochains, we have a diagram:

(8.9.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{G})_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DC_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch(\mathcal{H})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{*}}

where
L
,
B
and
P_{F}^{*}
are quasi-isomorphisms.
By introducing a cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{G}
we can modify Diagram
8.9.1
to get a new diagram:

\hbox to195.98pt{\vbox to64.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 97.98866pt\lower-25.92755pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

•

As before the maps
L_{\mathcal{E}}
and
B_{\mathcal{E}}
are the left and bottom inclusions of
\mathcal{E}
into its associated shift double structure
\mathcal{E}_{{\shift}}
.

•

The map
F_{{\shift}}^{\#}=\Id_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{shift}}}\otimes 1
is the “pullback comorphisms” obtained from pulling back the modules
\mathcal{E}_{{\shift}}
on the shift double groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
to the
F
-double groupoid via
F_{\shift}
.

•

The map
F^{\#}=\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes 1
is the pullback morphism along
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
.

•

The vertical arrow on the right side is obtained by taking the tensor product of the bottom inclusion for
\mathcal{E}
together with the pullback inclusion
P_{F}^{*}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\DC_{F}
. We leave it to the reader to verify that this is well-defined (i.e. compatible with the tensor products over
\Ch(\mathcal{G})
and
\Ch(\mathcal{G}_{\text{shift}})
).

We claim that if
\mathcal{E}
is good and
F
is a weak equivalence then
F^{\#}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

We have already seen that the bottom and left inclusions into the shift double complex are quasi-isomorphisms. If we can show
F^{\#}_{{\shift}}\circ L_{\mathcal{E}}
is a quasi-isomorphism and
B_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes P_{F}^{*}
is a quasi-isomorphism then we are finished. We will prove these facts in two separate lemmas.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

The map
B_{L}\otimes P_{F}^{*}
admits a left retract and is hence a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us fix
m\in\mathcal{H}^{(m)}
and consider the corresponding column of
\mathcal{E}_{{\shift}}\otimes\DC_{F}
as illustrated below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{(m+3)}\underset{\Ch(\mathcal{G})^{(m+3)}}{\otimes}\DC_{F}^{(2,m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{(m+2)}\underset{\Ch(\mathcal{G})^{(m+2)}}{\otimes}\DC_{F}^{(1,m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{(m+1)}\underset{\Ch(\mathcal{G})^{(m+1)}}{\otimes}\DC_{F}^{(0,m)}.}

In Lemma
6.6
, we constructed a left retract (of groupoids):

\{r^{n}\}_{\mathbb{N}}

of the
m
-column of
\DG_{F}
to
\mathcal{H}^{(m)}
along
P_{F}
. Notice that if we apply
F_{\shift}
to this left retract of groupoids we obtain the “standard” left retract of the
m
-column of
\mathcal{G}_{\shift}
to
\mathcal{G}^{(m)}
along
d^{m+1}_{m+1}
(see Example
4.4
):

\{s^{n+m}_{m}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(n+m)}\to\mathcal{G}^{(n+m+1)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

In Lemma
4.12
we also constructed the corresponding standard left retract for cosimplicial vector spaces:

\{\sigma^{n+m}_{m}\colon\mathcal{E}^{(m+n)}\to\mathcal{E}^{(m+n+1)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

of the
m
-column of
\mathcal{E}_{\shift}
to
\mathcal{E}^{(m)}
along
\phi^{m+1}_{m+1}
.
The standard left retract of groupoids is compatible with this left retract of modules in the sense that it is the “base map” for this left retract of modules. This compatibility allows us to conclude that the following is well defined (meaning that linear maps below are compatible with the relevant tensor product structure):

\{\theta^{n}\otimes(r^{n})^{*}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

Each component of the tensor product is already known to constitute a left retract. The
\theta
component is a left retract along
\phi^{m}_{m}
while the
(r^{n})^{*}
component is a left retract along
P_{F}
. Hence, the tensor product will also satisfy the left retract relations and will constitute a left retract along the bottom augmentation
B_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes P_{F}^{*}
. Since
B_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes P_{F}^{*}
is injective it must be a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

The map
F^{\#}_{\shift}\circ L_{\mathcal{E}}
admits a left retract and is hence a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and consider the
n
-th column of
\DG_{F}
,

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The map
L:=F^{\#}_{\text{shift}}\circ L_{\mathcal{E}}
corresponds to the map:

\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\to\mathcal{E}^{(n+1)}\underset{\Ch(\mathcal{G})^{(n+1)}}{\otimes}\DC_{F}^{(n,0)},\qquad e\mapsto\phi^{n+1}(e)\otimes 1.

The pair

(L,p)\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\overset{co}{\rightarrow}F^{*}_{\shift}\mathcal{E}

constitutes a module comorphism. According to Lemma
8.2
, if we can locally construct a left retraction along
(L,p)
then it follows that the cohomology of the
n
-th row is concentrated in the
0
-column and is equal to the image of
L
in degree zero. This would complete the proof.

Hence, it suffices to complete this module comorphism to a left retract locally in
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
. Similar to the proof of Theorem
8.3
, let
U\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
be an open subset which admits a local section:

(r^{0},\overline{f})\colon U\to\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{F_{0}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{F_{0}}}}}\mathcal{H}_{0},\qquad x\mapsto(r^{0}(x),\overline{f}(x)).

In the proof of Theorem
8.3
, we constructed a left retract
\{\rho^{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
for the restriction of the
n
-th row to
U^{(n)}
. We just need to promote this left retraction to a module comorphism.

Let
\{r^{k}\colon U^{(k)}\to U^{(k+1)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the associated target family induced by
r^{0}
. Since
\mathcal{E}
is assumed to be good, let

\{(r^{k})^{\#}\colon\mathcal{E}^{(k+1)}|_{U^{(k+1)}}\to\mathcal{E}^{(k)}|_{U^{(k)}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

be a lift of this target family to the level of the module.

We can put
\rho^{m}
together with the lifts of the target family to obtain a family of module comorphisms parameterized by
m\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\{\left(r^{n+1+m})^{\#}\otimes(\rho^{m})^{*},\rho^{m}\right)\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}

∎

9.
Cosimplicial Complexes over groupoids

9.1.
Triple Complexes

First, let us establish some terminology. Thus far, we have used the words “vertical” and “horizontal” to refer positions/directions relative to the written page. We will now use the word “normal” to refer to the direction orthogonal to the page.

We think of our triple (cochain) complexes as being bounded below by zero in each index. These triple complexes
E^{\bullet,\bullet,\bullet}
are equipped with three commuting differentials:

d_{V}\colon E^{\bullet,\bullet,\bullet}\to E^{\bullet+1,\bullet,\bullet}

d_{H}\colon E^{\bullet,\bullet,\bullet}\to E^{\bullet,\bullet+1,\bullet}

d_{N}\colon E^{\bullet,\bullet,\bullet}\to E^{\bullet,\bullet,\bullet+1}

These differentials will be called the vertical, horizontal, and normal differentials (respectively).

The most important cohomology (and the one we are most interested in computing) is the analogue of the total cohomology.

Definition 9.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a triple complex. The total complex of a triple complex is defined such that the
k
th homogeneous component is given by:

\mathcal{E}_{tot}^{k}:=\sum_{a+b+c=k}\mathcal{E}^{a,b,c}.

The total differential is defined so that on the subspace
\mathcal{E}^{a,b,c}
we have:

d_{tot}|_{\mathcal{E}^{a,b,c}}=d_{V}+(-1)^{a}d_{H}+(-1)^{(a+b)}d_{N}.

A routine check verifies that
d_{tot}
squares to zero.

Triple complexes can be thought of as double complexes
D^{(n,m)}
where each
D^{(n,m)}
is itself a complex rather than a vector space. However, it is not true that the total cohomology of the triple complex can be computed by first computing the total cohomology of
D^{(n,m)}
. For this reason we are better off treating the topic in a way that does not have any “privileged” directions.

Despite the previous remark, there is still a version of the augmentation lemma for triple complexes. In order to state this properly we should define augmentations in the context of triple complexes.

Definition 9.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a triple complex and
D
is a double complex. A
left-normal augmentation
(LN-augmentation)

LN\colon D\to\mathcal{E}

consists of a morphism of double complexes:

LN\colon V\to D^{\bullet,0,\bullet}

with the property that
d_{H}L=0
.

Bottom-normal and left-bottom augmentations are defined similarly (each just corresponds to changing which part of the triple complex is set to zero.)

We say a left-normal augmentation is
acyclic
if for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that the following augmented complex is exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{m,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{m,0,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{m,1,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{H}}

Let us now state the augmentation lemma for triple complexes.

Lemma 9.3
.

Suppose
E
is a triple complex and suppose
LN\colon D\to E
is an acyclic left-normal augmentation of
E
.

Then
LN
induces an isomorphism
L_{*}\colon H(D)\to H(E)
.

We will not include a proof of this lemma here. Instead, we will simply remark that this fact can be proved as a corollary of the two-dimensional augmentation lemma. The idea is to collapse the triple complex by “combining” the left-normal direction into a single complex to view
E
as a double complex instead.

Of course, by symmetrical arguments, one has corresponding versions of the augmentation lemma for left-bottom and bottom-normal augmentations.

One should also take note of the fact that determining whether an augmentation is acyclic really only depends on the exactness of the one dimensional complexes orthogonal to the augmentation.

The following fact is rather clear from the definition but it is worth pointing out. Roughly, it says that, to check if an augmentation is acyclic, one only needs to verify this for each “level” of the normal direction.

Lemma 9.4
.

Suppose
E
is a triple complex and
LN\colon D\to E
is a left-normal augmentation. Then
E
is acyclic if and only if, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that the associated augmentation of double complexes:

LN^{\bullet,n}\colon D^{\bullet,n}\to E^{\bullet,\bullet,n}

is acyclic.

There is nothing particularly special about the normal direction here. Similar facts can be established for various permutations of fixed indices and types of augmentations. However, the above form will turn out to be the one most relevant to our arguments in the next section.

9.2.
Cosimplicial complexes

Definition 9.5
.

A
cosimplicial complex
over a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
consists of a cosimplicial module
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{G}
that is further equipped with the structure of a cochain complex on each level of the nerve.

Therefore, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have a (cochain) complex of the form:

\mathcal{E}^{(n)}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{E}^{(n)})^{k},\qquad(d_{N}^{(n)})^{k}\colon(\mathcal{E}^{(n)})^{k}\to(\mathcal{E}^{(n)})^{k+1}.

Furthermore, we require that this additional structure is compatible with the cosimplicial module structure. In other words, all coface maps are morphisms of complexes.

A cosimplicial complex
\mathcal{E}
over a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is said to be
good
if each homogeneous component
\mathcal{E}^{k}
is
good
as a cosimplicial module over
\mathcal{G}

Notation 9.6
.

Since complexes include an additional index, we adopt the notation
\mathcal{E}^{n,k}:=(\mathcal{E}^{(n)})^{k}
to denote the homogeneous components.

For reasons that will be clear later, we will think of the differential
d_{N}
as corresponding to a direction “normal” to the page (hence the
N
subscript).

Our model example is the differential forms of the nerve:

Example 9.7

(Bott-Schulman Double Complex)
.

Given a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, write
\Omega(\mathcal{G})
to denote the cosimplicial module of differential forms in all degrees. So:

\Omega(\mathcal{G})^{n,i}:=\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)}).

The differential
D
is the standard de Rham differential.

Observe that if the compatibility of the face maps with
d_{N}
implies that
d_{N}
commutes with the simplicial differential. Hence,
\mathcal{E}
actually has the structure of a double complex.

Definition 9.8
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a cosimplicial complex over
\mathcal{G}
. The cohomology of
\mathcal{E}
, written
H(\mathcal{E})
is defined to be the
total
cohomology of
\mathcal{E}
regarded as a double complex.

Observe that the shifted complex
\mathcal{E}_{\shift}
of a cosimplicial complex over
\mathcal{G}
is no longer just a double complex but a triple complex since we have one more differential
d_{N}
which operates in the normal direction.

9.3.
Morita invariance for pullbacks

We will now prove the analogue of Theorem
8.9
except in the context of cosimplicial complexes rather than modules.

Before proceeding with the statement, we remark that cosimplicial complexes can be pulled back along groupoid homomorphisms. The definition is the same as the definition for cosimplicial modules:

Given
\mathcal{E}
a cosimplicial complex on
\mathcal{G}
and a groupoid homomorphism
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
then:

(F^{*}\mathcal{E})^{m,n}:=\mathcal{E}^{(m,n)}\otimes_{C^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}^{(m)})}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}^{(m)}).

Theorem 9.9
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a good cosimplicial complex over
\mathcal{G}
and
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence of Lie groupoids. Then the natural map:

F^{\#}\colon\mathcal{E}\to F^{*}\mathcal{E},\qquad e\mapsto e\otimes 1

is an isomorphism in cohomology.

Proof.

This theorem is essentially a corollary of the same result for good cosimplicial modules. To see why, let us recall how the proof for the case of a cosimplicial module went. First, we consider the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DG_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

which relates
\mathcal{G}
to the shift double groupoid and the double groupoid associated to
F
. Using
\mathcal{E}
, we construct a closely related diagram of complexes:

(9.9.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\#}_{\shift}\mathcal{E}_{\shift}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LN^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BN^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}^{\#}}

Now, in the setting where
\mathcal{E}
is a cosimplicial complex, we see that
\mathcal{E}_{\shift}
and
F^{*}_{\shift}\mathcal{E}_{\shift}
are now
triple complexes
. Furthermore,
LN^{\#}
is a left-normal augmentation.
BN^{\#}
and
P_{F}^{\#}
are bottom-normal augmentations.

If we restrict these complexes to one level of the “normal” direction then this is the same diagram that appears in the proof of the cosimplicial modules case (Theorem
8.9
). Furthermore, in that same proof we established that all of the augmentations in this diagram are acyclic. In Lemma
9.4
we observed that the property of being acyclic can be verified by restricting to one level of the normal direction. Therefore, it follows that the following augmentations are acyclic:

LN^{\#},BN^{\#},P_{F}^{\#},F^{\#}_{\shift}\circ LN^{\#}

In particular, they are all quasi-isomorphisms. From this it follows that
F^{\#}_{\shift}
is a quasi-isomorphism and hence
F^{\#}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

9.4.
Morita Invariance for the big site

We will now prove the analogue of Theorem
8.9
in the context of cosimplicial complexes.

To state the theorem properly, we require the notion of a sheaf of complexes on the big site.

Definition 9.10
.

A
sheaf of complexes on the big site
consists of a sheaf of modules
\mathcal{E}
on the big site that is further enriched with a compatible sheaf of complexes structure.

Given such a sheaf, every Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is equipped with a cosimplicial complex
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
and a corresponding cohomology
H(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}})
.

If
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a homomorphism of Lie groupoids we write
\mathcal{E}_{F}\colon\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}
to denote the associated morphism of cosimplicial complexes.

Theorem 9.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a sheaf of complexes on the big site.
F\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a weak equivalence of Lie groupoids, then the map:

\mathcal{E}_{F}\colon\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}

induces an isomorphism in cohomology.

Proof.

This theorem is essentially a corollary of the proof for cosimplicial modules (Theorem
8.9
). To see why, let us recall how the proof for the case of a cosimplicial module went. First, we consider the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{{\shift}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DG_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{{\shift}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

which relates
\mathcal{G}
to the shift double groupoid and the double groupoid associated to
F
. We can apply our functor
\mathcal{E}
to this entire diagram to obtain a new diagram of complexes:

\hbox to178.69pt{\vbox to65.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 89.26096pt\lower-27.89166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-89.26096pt}{-22.73193pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -78.81 27.53 L -46.71 27.53}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-33.55681pt}{19.89864pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-53.44507pt}{23.25696pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Observe that in this case we are now dealing with triple complexes and augmentations of triple complexes. Now if we restrict the above diagram to a single level of the normal direction (say
n\in\mathbb{N}
), it becomes precisely the diagram appearing in the proof of Theorem
8.9
except applied to the functor
\mathcal{E}^{n}
which only considers a single homogeneous degree of our sheaf of complexes.

In the same proof, we showed that all of the augmentations appearing in the 2D version of the above diagram are acyclic. We observed in Lemma
9.4
that it is sufficient to check that an augmentation is acyclic for individual levels of the normal direction. Hence, it follows that all augmentations (of triple complexes) that appear in the above diagram are quasi-isomorphisms. From this, it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{F_{\shift}}
is a quasi-isomorphism and so
\mathcal{E}_{F}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎
